{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01YEgf7mf6c5EfGu63Y3EfnH","type":"tool_result","content":"   740→class StandardScaler(OneToOneFeatureMixin, TransformerMixin, BaseEstimator):\n   741→    \"\"\"Standardize features by removing the mean and scaling to unit variance.\n   742→\n   743→    The standard score of a sample `x` is calculated as:\n   744→\n   745→    .. code-block:: text\n   746→\n   747→        z = (x - u) / s\n   748→\n   749→    where `u` is the mean of the training samples or zero if `with_mean=False`,\n   750→    and `s` is the standard deviation of the training samples or one if\n   751→    `with_std=False`.\n   752→\n   753→    Centering and scaling happen independently on each feature by computing\n   754→    the relevant statistics on the samples in the training set. Mean and\n   755→    standard deviation are then stored to be used on later data using\n   756→    :meth:`transform`.\n   757→\n   758→    Standardization of a dataset is a common requirement for many\n   759→    machine learning estimators: they might behave badly if the\n   760→    individual features do not more or less look like standard normally\n   761→    distributed data (e.g. Gaussian with 0 mean and unit variance).\n   762→\n   763→    For instance many elements used in the objective function of\n   764→    a learning algorithm (such as the RBF kernel of Support Vector\n   765→    Machines or the L1 and L2 regularizers of linear models) assume that\n   766→    all features are centered around 0 and have variance in the same\n   767→    order. If a feature has a variance that is orders of magnitude larger\n   768→    than others, it might dominate the objective function and make the\n   769→    estimator unable to learn from other features correctly as expected.\n   770→\n   771→    `StandardScaler` is sensitive to outliers, and the features may scale\n   772→    differently from each other in the presence of outliers. For an example\n   773→    visualization, refer to :ref:`Compare StandardScaler with other scalers\n   774→    <plot_all_scaling_standard_scaler_section>`.\n   775→\n   776→    This scaler can also be applied to sparse CSR or CSC matrices by passing\n   777→    `with_mean=False` to avoid breaking the sparsity structure of the data.\n   778→\n   779→    Read more in the :ref:`User Guide <preprocessing_scaler>`.\n   780→\n   781→    Parameters\n   782→    ----------\n   783→    copy : bool, default=True\n   784→        If False, try to avoid a copy and do inplace scaling instead.\n   785→        This is not guaranteed to always work inplace; e.g. if the data is\n   786→        not a NumPy array or scipy.sparse CSR matrix, a copy may still be\n   787→        returned.\n   788→\n   789→    with_mean : bool, default=True\n   790→        If True, center the data before scaling.\n   791→        This does not work (and will raise an exception) when attempted on\n   792→        sparse matrices, because centering them entails building a dense\n   793→        matrix which in common use cases is likely to be too large to fit in\n   794→        memory.\n   795→\n   796→    with_std : bool, default=True\n   797→        If True, scale the data to unit variance (or equivalently,\n   798→        unit standard deviation).\n   799→\n   800→    Attributes\n   801→    ----------\n   802→    scale_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n   803→        Per feature relative scaling of the data to achieve zero mean and unit\n   804→        variance. Generally this is calculated using `np.sqrt(var_)`. If a\n   805→        variance is zero, we can't achieve unit variance, and the data is left\n   806→        as-is, giving a scaling factor of 1. `scale_` is equal to `None`\n   807→        when `with_std=False`.\n   808→\n   809→        .. versionadded:: 0.17\n   810→           *scale_*\n   811→\n   812→    mean_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n   813→        The mean value for each feature in the training set.\n   814→        Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and ``with_std=False``.\n   815→\n   816→    var_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n   817→        The variance for each feature in the training set. Used to compute\n   818→        `scale_`. Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and\n   819→        ``with_std=False``.\n   820→\n   821→    n_features_in_ : int\n   822→        Number of features seen during :term:`fit`.\n   823→\n   824→        .. versionadded:: 0.24\n   825→\n   826→    feature_names_in_ : ndarray of shape (`n_features_in_`,)\n   827→        Names of features seen during :term:`fit`. Defined only when `X`\n   828→        has feature names that are all strings.\n   829→\n   830→        .. versionadded:: 1.0\n   831→\n   832→    n_samples_seen_ : int or ndarray of shape (n_features,)\n   833→        The number of samples processed by the estimator for each feature.\n   834→        If there are no missing samples, the ``n_samples_seen`` will be an\n   835→        integer, otherwise it will be an array of dtype int. If\n   836→        `sample_weights` are used it will be a float (if no missing data)\n   837→        or an array of dtype float that sums the weights seen so far.\n   838→        Will be reset on new calls to fit, but increments across\n   839→        ``partial_fit`` calls.\n   840→\n   841→    See Also\n   842→    --------\n   843→    scale : Equivalent function without the estimator API.\n   844→\n   845→    :class:`~sklearn.decomposition.PCA` : Further removes the linear\n   846→        correlation across features with 'whiten=True'.\n   847→\n   848→    Notes\n   849→    -----\n   850→    NaNs are treated as missing values: disregarded in fit, and maintained in\n   851→    transform.\n   852→\n   853→    We use a biased estimator for the standard deviation, equivalent to\n   854→    `numpy.std(x, ddof=0)`. Note that the choice of `ddof` is unlikely to\n   855→    affect model performance.\n   856→\n   857→    Examples\n   858→    --------\n   859→    >>> from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler\n   860→    >>> data = [[0, 0], [0, 0], [1, 1], [1, 1]]\n   861→    >>> scaler = StandardScaler()\n   862→    >>> print(scaler.fit(data))\n   863→    StandardScaler()\n   864→    >>> print(scaler.mean_)\n   865→    [0.5 0.5]\n   866→    >>> print(scaler.transform(data))\n   867→    [[-1. -1.]\n   868→     [-1. -1.]\n   869→     [ 1.  1.]\n   870→     [ 1.  1.]]\n   871→    >>> print(scaler.transform([[2, 2]]))\n   872→    [[3. 3.]]\n   873→    \"\"\"\n   874→\n   875→    _parameter_constraints: dict = {\n   876→        \"copy\": [\"boolean\"],\n   877→        \"with_mean\": [\"boolean\"],\n   878→        \"with_std\": [\"boolean\"],\n   879→    }\n   880→\n   881→    def __init__(self, *, copy=True, with_mean=True, with_std=True):\n   882→        self.with_mean = with_mean\n   883→        self.with_std = with_std\n   884→        self.copy = copy\n   885→\n   886→    def _reset(self):\n   887→        \"\"\"Reset internal data-dependent state of the scaler, if necessary.\n   888→\n   889→        __init__ parameters are not touched.\n   890→        \"\"\"\n   891→        # Mean and\n    standard deviation are then stored to be used on later data using\n    :meth:`transform`.\n\n    Standardization of a dataset is a common requirement for many\n    machine learning estimators: they might behave badly if the\n    individual features do not more or less look like standard normally\n    distributed data (e.g. Gaussian with 0 mean and unit variance).\n\n    For instance many elements used in the objective function of\n    a learning algorithm (such as the RBF kernel of Support Vector\n    Machines or the L1 and L2 regularizers of linear models) assume that\n    all features are centered around 0 and have variance in the same\n    order. If a feature has a variance that is orders of magnitude larger\n    than others, it might dominate the objective function and make the\n    estimator unable to learn from other features correctly as expected.\n\n    `StandardScaler` is sensitive to outliers, and the features may scale\n    differently from each other in the presence of outliers. For an example\n    visualization, refer to :ref:`Compare StandardScaler with other scalers\n    <plot_all_scaling_standard_scaler_section>`.\n\n    This scaler can also be applied to sparse CSR or CSC matrices by passing\n    `with_mean=False` to avoid breaking the sparsity structure of the data.\n\n    Read more in the :ref:`User Guide <preprocessing_scaler>`.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    copy : bool, default=True\n        If False, try to avoid a copy and do inplace scaling instead.\n        This is not guaranteed to always work inplace; e.g. if the data is\n        not a NumPy array or scipy.sparse CSR matrix, a copy may still be\n        returned.\n\n    with_mean : bool, default=True\n        If True, center the data before scaling.\n        This does not work (and will raise an exception) when attempted on\n        sparse matrices, because centering them entails building a dense\n        matrix which in common use cases is likely to be too large to fit in\n        memory.\n\n    with_std : bool, default=True\n        If True, scale the data to unit variance (or equivalently,\n        unit standard deviation).\n\n    Attributes\n    ----------\n    scale_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n        Per feature relative scaling of the data to achieve zero mean and unit\n        variance. Generally this is calculated using `np.sqrt(var_)`. If a\n        variance is zero, we can't achieve unit variance, and the data is left\n        as-is, giving a scaling factor of 1. `scale_` is equal to `None`\n        when `with_std=False`.\n\n        .. versionadded:: 0.17\n           *scale_*\n\n    mean_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n        The mean value for each feature in the training set.\n        Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and ``with_std=False``.\n\n    var_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n        The variance for each feature in the training set. Used to compute\n        `scale_`. Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and\n        ``with_std=False``.\n\n    n_features_in_ : int\n        Number of features seen during :term:`fit`.\n\n        .. versionadded:: 0.24\n\n    feature_names_in_ : ndarray of shape (`n_features_in_`,)\n        Names of features seen during :term:`fit`. Defined only when `X`\n        has feature names that are all strings.\n\n        .. versionadded:: 1.0\n\n    n_samples_seen_ : int or ndarray of shape (n_features,)\n        The number of samples processed by the estimator for each feature.\n        If there are no missing samples, the ``n_samples_seen`` will be an\n        integer, otherwise it will be an array of dtype int. If\n        `sample_weights` are used it will be a float (if no missing data)\n        or an array of dtype float that sums the weights seen so far.\n        Will be reset on new calls to fit, but increments across\n        ``partial_fit`` calls.\n\n    See Also\n    --------\n    scale : Equivalent function without the estimator API.\n\n    :class:`~sklearn.decomposition.PCA` : Further removes the linear\n        correlation across features with 'whiten=True'.\n\n    Notes\n    -----\n    NaNs are treated as missing values: disregarded in fit, and maintained in\n    transform.\n\n    We use a biased estimator for the standard deviation, equivalent to\n    `numpy.std(x, ddof=0)`. Note that the choice of `ddof` is unlikely to\n    affect model performance.\n\n    Examples\n    --------\n    >>> from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler\n    >>> data = [[0, 0], [0, 0], [1, 1], [1, 1]]\n    >>> scaler = StandardScaler()\n    >>> print(scaler.fit(data))\n    StandardScaler()\n    >>> print(scaler.mean_)\n    [0.5 0.5]\n    >>> print(scaler.transform(data))\n    [[-1. -1.]\n     [-1. -1.]\n     [ 1.  1.]\n     [ 1.  1.]]\n    >>> print(scaler.transform([[2, 2]]))\n    [[3. 3.]]\n    \"\"\"\n\n    _parameter_constraints: dict = {\n        \"copy\": [\"boolean\"],\n        \"with_mean\": [\"boolean\"],\n        \"with_std\": [\"boolean\"],\n    }\n\n    def __init__(self, *, copy=True, with_mean=True, with_std=True):\n        self.with_mean = with_mean\n        self.with_std = with_std\n        self.copy = copy\n\n    def _reset(self):\n        \"\"\"Reset internal data-dependent state of the scaler, if necessary.\n\n        __init__ parameters are not touched.\n        \"\"\"\n        # See docstring for motivation and alternatives.\"\n                )\n            sparse_constructor = (\n                sparse.csr_matrix if X.format == \"csr\" else sparse.csc_matrix\n            )\n\n            if self.with_std:\n                # First pass\n                if not hasattr(self, \"scale_\"):\n                    self.mean_, self.var_, self.n_samples_seen_ = mean_variance_axis(\n                        X, axis=0, weights=sample_weight, return_sum_weights=True\n                    )\n                # Next passes\n                else:\n                    (\n                        self.mean_,\n                        self.var_,\n                        self.n_samples_seen_,\n                    ) = incr_mean_variance_axis(\n                        X,\n                        axis=0,\n                        last_mean=self.mean_,\n                        last_var=self.var_,\n                        last_n=self.n_samples_seen_,\n                        weights=sample_weight,\n                    )\n                # We force the mean and variance to float64 for large arrays\n                # See https://github.com/scikit-learn/scikit-learn/pull/12338\n                self.mean_ = self.mean_.astype(np.float64, copy=False)\n                self.var_ = self.var_.astype(np.float64, copy=False)\n            else:\n                self.mean_ = None  # as with_mean must be False for sparse\n                self.var_ = None\n                weights = _check_sample_weight(sample_weight, X)\n                sum_weights_nan = weights @ sparse_constructor(\n                    (np.isnan(X.data), X.indices, X.indptr), shape=X.shape\n                )\n                self.n_samples_seen_ += (np.sum(weights) - sum_weights_nan).astype(\n                    dtype\n                )\n        else:\n            # First pass\n            if not hasattr(self, \"scale_\"):\n                self.mean_ = 0.0\n                if self.with_std:\n                    self.var_ = 0.0\n                else:\n                    self.var_ = None\n\n            if not self.with_mean and not self.with_std:\n                self.mean_ = None\n                self.var_ = None\n                self.n_samples_seen_ += X.shape[0] - xp.isnan(X).sum(axis=0)\n\n            else:\n                self.mean_, self.var_, self.n_samples_seen_ = _incremental_mean_and_var(\n                    X,\n                    self.mean_,\n                    self.var_,\n                    self.n_samples_seen_,\n                    sample_weight=sample_weight,\n                )\n\n        # for backward-compatibility, reduce n_samples_seen_ to an integer\n        # if the number of samples is the same for each feature (i.e. no\n        # missing values)\n        if xp.max(self.n_samples_seen_) == xp.min(self.n_samples_seen_):\n            self.n_samples_seen_ = self.n_samples_seen_[0]\n\n        if self.with_std:\n            # Extract the list of near constant features on the raw variances,\n            # before taking the square root.\n            constant_mask = _is_constant_feature(\n                self.var_, self.mean_, self.n_samples_seen_\n            )\n            self.scale_ = _handle_zeros_in_scale(\n                xp.sqrt(self.var_), copy=False, constant_mask=constant_mask\n            )\n        else:\n            self.scale_ = None\n\n        return self\n\n    def transform(self, X, copy=None):","numLines":150,"startLine":926,"totalLines":3758}}}

Values\n          cannot be infinite.\n\n        .. versionadded:: 1.6\n           `force_all_finite` was renamed to `ensure_all_finite`.\n\n    ensure_non_negative : bool, default=False\n        Make sure the array has only non-negative values. The\n        # result is that np.array(..) produces an array of complex dtype\n        # and we need to catch and raise exception for such cases.\n        _ensure_no_complex_data(array)\n\n        if ensure_2d:\n            # If input is scalar raise error\n            if array.ndim == 0:\n                raise ValueError(\n                    \"Expected 2D array, got scalar array instead:\\narray={}.\\n\"\n                    \"Reshape your data either using array.reshape(-1, 1) if \"\n                    \"your data has a single feature or array.reshape(1, -1) \"\n                    \"if it contains a single sample.\".format(array)\n                )\n            # If input is 1D raise error\n            if array.ndim == 1:\n                # If input is a Series-like object (eg. pandas Series or polars Series)\n                if type_if_series is not None:\n                    msg = (\n                        f\"Expected a 2-dimensional container but got {type_if_series} \"\n                        \"instead. Pandas Nullable Integer to NumPy Conversion\n\n**File:** `pandas/core/arrays/masked.py`\n\n**`__array__` method (lines 694-716):**\n```python\ndef __array__(\n    self, dtype: NpDtype | None = None, copy: bool | None = None\n) -> np.ndarray:\n    \"\"\"\n    the array interface, return my values\n    We return an object array here to preserve our scalar values\n    \"\"\"\n    if copy is False:\n        if not self._hasna:\n            result = np.array(self._data, dtype=dtype, copy=copy)\n            # ...\n            return result\n        raise ValueError(\n            \"Unable to avoid copy while creating an array as requested.\"\n        )\n\n    if copy is None:\n        copy = False\n    return self.to_numpy(dtype=dtype, copy=copy)  # Line 716\n```\n\n**`to_numpy` method (lines 512-606):**\n```python\ndef to_numpy(self, dtype: npt.DTypeLike | None = None, ...):\n    hasna = self._hasna\n    dtype, na_value = to_numpy_dtype_inference(self, dtype, na_value, hasna)  # Line 579\n    # ...\n```\n\n### 4. ROOT CAUSE: Pandas dtype Inference Logic\n\n**File:** `pandas/core/arrays/_utils.py`\n\n**Lines 41-50 (ROOT CAUSE):**\n```python\ndef to_numpy_dtype_inference(\n    arr: ExtensionArray,\n    dtype: npt.DTypeLike | None,\n    na_value,\n    hasna: bool,\n) -> tuple[np.dtype | None, Any]:\n    # ...\n    if dtype is None and is_numeric_dtype(arr.dtype):\n        inferred_numeric_dtype = True\n        if hasna:\n            if arr.dtype.kind == \"b\":\n                result_dtype = np.dtype(np.object_)\n            else:\n                if arr.dtype.kind in \"iu\":  # Line 41: Integer types\n                    result_dtype = np.dtype(np.float64)  # Line 42\n                else:\n                    result_dtype = arr.dtype.numpy_dtype\n                if na_value is lib.no_default:\n                    if not is_nan_na():  # Line 46: Configuration check\n                        na_value = NA  # Line 47\n                        dtype = np.dtype(object)  # Line 48: FORCES OBJECT DTYPE\n                    else:\n                        na_value = np.nan  # Line 50\n```\n\n**The Bug:**\nWhen `is_nan_na()` returns `False` (the default pandas configuration where `pd.NA` and `np.nan` are distinguished), lines 47-48 override the previously set `result_dtype = np.dtype(np.float64)` by setting `dtype = np.dtype(object)`.
